=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer eventTime denoting the duration of an event, where the event occurs from time t = 0 to time t = eventTime.
You are also given two integer arrays startTime and endTime, each of length n. These represent the start and end time of n non-overlapping meetings, where the i^th meeting occurs during the time [startTime[i], endTime[i]].
You can reschedule at most k meetings by moving their start time while maintaining the same duration, to maximize the longest continuous period of free time during the event.
The relative order of all the meetings should stay the same and they should remain non-overlapping.
Return the maximum amount of free time possible after rearranging the meetings.
Note that the meetings can not be rescheduled to a time outside the event.
 
Example 1:

Input: eventTime = 5, k = 1, startTime = [1,3], endTime = [2,5]
Output: 2
Explanation:

Reschedule the meeting at [1, 2] to [2, 3], leaving no meetings during the time [0, 2].

Example 2:

Input: eventTime = 10, k = 1, startTime = [0,2,9], endTime = [1,4,10]
Output: 6
Explanation:

Reschedule the meeting at [2, 4] to [1, 3], leaving no meetings during the time [3, 9].

Example 3:

Input: eventTime = 5, k = 2, startTime = [0,1,2,3,4], endTime = [1,2,3,4,5]
Output: 0
Explanation:
There is no time during the event not occupied by meetings.

 
Constraints:

1 <= eventTime <= 10^9
n == startTime.length == endTime.length
2 <= n <= 10^5
1 <= k <= n
0 <= startTime[i] < endTime[i] <= eventTime
endTime[i] <= startTime[i + 1] where i lies in the range [0, n - 2].

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maxFreeTime(self, eventTime: int, k: int, startTime: List[int], endTime: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Implementation:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxFreeTime(self, eventTime: int, k: int, startTime: List[int], endTime: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(startTime)
        gaps = [0]*(n+1)
        gaps[0] = startTime[0]
        for i in range(1,n):
            gaps[i] = startTime[i] - endTime[i-1]
        gaps[n] = eventTime - endTime[-1]
        
        w = k + 1
        cur = sum(gaps[:w])
        ans = cur
        for i in range(w, n+1):
            cur += gaps[i] - gaps[i-w]
            if cur > ans:
                ans = cur
        return ans
```
Need if w > len(gaps)? k<=n => w<=n+1, len=n+1, okay. If n maybe 0? constraints n>=2, okay.

Could optimize memory by not storing all gaps? Need sliding window, can compute gaps list. n=1e5, fine.

Example2 eventTime=10,k=1,start=[0,2,9],end=[1,4,10], gaps[0]=0, gaps[1]=2-1=1, gaps[2]=9-4=5, gaps[3]=0, w=2, max 6. return6.
Example3 eventTime=5,k=2,start=[0,1,2,3,4],end=[1,2,3,4,5], gaps all0, w=3, ans0.

Good.

Now, let's revisit the proof of upper bound in more detail to ensure no hidden issue with moving at most k meetings but not necessarily all moved meetings adjacent to target gap. Suppose final gap G_i between meeting i and i+1. Let S be set of moved meetings. We want show G_i <= max window of k+1 gaps. We can use a simpler combinatorial argument perhaps: The final gap G_i is the distance between end of meeting i and start of meeting i+1. In original timeline, between these two meetings there is original gap f_i. The difference G_i - f_i = shift_right(i+1) - shift_left(i). To increase the gap, we need move meeting i left and/or meeting i+1 right. Moving meeting i left by X requires moving a contiguous block of meetings ending at i to the left; each moved meeting can contribute at most one adjacent original gap? Actually if a meeting is moved left, it can "absorb" one gap to its left. The total left shift of meeting i is at most sum of original gaps between meeting i and the nearest unmoved meeting to the left (or start). If there are m moved meetings in the block ending at i, that's at most m original gaps. Similarly right shift of meeting i+1 at most b original gaps. Thus G_i <= f_i + (number of moved meetings to right) gaps + (number to left) gaps, total <= k+1 gaps. This is intuitive.

Let's test with a scenario where a moved meeting not adjacent to target gap but shifted could indirectly allow meeting i to shift more without being in contiguous block? Suppose moved meeting i-2 left, meeting i-1 unmoved. Does that help shift meeting i left? No, because meeting i-1 unmoved blocks; gap between i-2 and i-1 might increase? Let's see if M_{i-2} moves left, gap between M_{i-2} and M_{i-1} increases, but M_{i-1} remains fixed. The gap between M_{i-1} and M_i can still shrink at most f_{i-1} if M_i moved left. M_{i-2} movement doesn't affect M_i because M_{i-1} fixed. So only contiguous block of moved meetings immediately adjacent to i matters. Similarly, moved meetings on both sides of target gap are separated by the gap; total moved count includes both blocks. Thus upper bound holds.

What if meeting i itself is not moved but meeting i+1 moved right; then G_i = f_i + right_shift. Right_shift of i+1 by moving a block of b meetings starting at i+1 can be sum of b gaps to the right. Then G_i = f_i + sum of b gaps = window of b+1 gaps. b<=k. Good. If both i and i+1 moved, left block count a includes meeting i, right block count b includes meeting i+1, total a+b<=k. G_i = f_i + sum_{right gaps} + sum_{left gaps}; count a+b+1<=k+1. Good.

Boundary gap before first: G_0 = f_0 + delta_1. To increase, move a block starting at 1 right; delta_1 = sum of b gaps f_1..f_b. G_0 = sum f_0..f_b, count b+1<=k+1. Similarly after last.

Thus algorithm solid.

Let's consider if the final gap after moving a block of k meetings to one side might be larger than sum of k+1 gaps because we can also move some meetings outside the block to create extra space adjacent? But final gaps are only between consecutive meetings. If we move meetings outside the block, they might increase other gaps, not the target gap. The target gap's boundaries are the moved block and the fixed meeting; moving outside doesn't affect it. Could moving meetings outside allow the block to shift further without increasing moved count? No, the block's shift is constrained by fixed boundary; outside moves don't help because fixed boundary remains. If we choose not to leave a fixed boundary, e.g., move all meetings on one side too, then the target gap might be between two moved blocks; but then the set of moved meetings adjacent to gap could be larger, and total moved count still k. The formula with left block and right block covers both boundaries moved; if all meetings on one side moved up to boundary, the block extends to event boundary and can shift by sum of all gaps to boundary, count moved meetings = gaps count. Still within k. Example k=n, all meetings moved, gap after all meetings = sum all gaps. Good.

Is it possible to create gap f0+f2 by moving one meeting? Original two meetings M1,M2. Final gaps: before M1 = f0 + delta1, between = f1 + delta2 - delta1, after = f2 - delta2. At most one nonzero delta. If move M1 (delta1 !=0, delta2=0): gaps: f0+delta1, f1-delta1, f2. Max maybe if delta1 negative, before gap decreases, between increases; max between = f1 + (-delta1) <= f1+f0 (since delta1>=-f0). If delta1 positive, before increases <=f0+f1, between decreases. So max f0+f1 or f2. If move M2: max f1+f2 or f0. So formula. Good.

n=3, k=1. Formula max adjacent gap sums f0+f1, f1+f2, f2+f3. Could moving M1 create gap after M2? Move M1 right, before gap f0+f1, between M1-M2 maybe f1-delta? Actually if delta1 positive, before gap increases, between decreases. Max before f0+f1. Move M2, between M1-M2 and M2-M3 combine? If delta2 negative, gap before M2 increases = f1 + (-delta2) <= f1+f1? Wait constraints with M1 unmoved: delta2 >= -f1, so gap before M2 = f1+delta2? Let's compute final gap between M1 and M2 = f1 + delta2 - delta1 (delta1=0). If delta2 negative, gap = f1 + delta2, decreases. Wait to increase gap before M2, need delta2 positive? Actually gap between M1 and M2 = final_start_M2 - final_end_M1 = (s2+delta2) - e1 = f1 + delta2. So to increase gap before M2, move M2 right (delta2 positive). But moving M2 right may be constrained by M3; if M3 unmoved, delta2 <= f2. Then gap before M2 <= f1+f2. That combines f1 and f2? It is sum of f1+f2, yes. If move M2 left, gap after M2 increases: gap between M2 and M3 = f2 - delta2 <= f1+f2. So moving one meeting can increase either adjacent gap by sum of the two gaps around it. Good. Earlier I thought shift left for target gap after meeting i; for gap between i and i+1, to increase, move i left or i+1 right. For gap before M2 (i=1), move M2 right. Good. Our block formula for target gap between i and i+1: left block ending at i (move meeting i left) or right block starting at i+1 (move meeting i+1 right). For gap before M2, i=1, right block M2 right shift by f2 gives gap f1+f2. Good.

Before final, let's double-check if there are any hidden constraints around "relative order of all the meetings should stay the same" and "meetings can not be rescheduled to a time outside the event." Our construction moving block to one side maintains all meetings within event because the block is placed within the span between fixed boundaries. Need if window f_a..f_{a+k}, move meetings a+1..a+k to left adjacent to meeting a (or start). Let's compute final positions:
- For j = a+1..a+k, set final_start_j = end_a + sum_{t=a+1}^{j-1} d_t (if a=0, end_a=0). Then final_end_j = end_a + sum_{t=a+1}^{j} d_t.
- Meeting a+k+1 remains at original start S_{a+k+1} (or eventTime if a+k=n). Need ensure final_end_{a+k} <= S_{a+k+1}. Original S_{a+k+1} = end_a + f_a + d_{a+1} + f_{a+1}+...+d_{a+k}+f_{a+k}. Final_end_block = end_a + sum_{t=a+1}^{a+k} d_t. Difference = f_a+...+f_{a+k} >=0. So valid. If a=0, end_a=0; original S_{k+1} = f0+d1+...+dk+fk; final_end_block = sum d; valid. If a+k=n, S_{n+1}=eventTime; original eventTime = end_a + f_a + d_{a+1}+...+d_n+f_n; final_end_block = end_a+sum d; difference = sum gaps >=0. So within event. Good.
If we instead move block to right adjacent to meeting a+k+1, final_start_j = S_{a+k+1} - sum_{t=j}^{a+k} d_t; need final_start_{a+1} >= end_a. Difference = sum gaps >=0. Valid. So construction works.

The accepted solution: Create `freeTime` array of length n+1 with startTime[0], startTime[i]-endTime[i-1], eventTime-endTime[-1]. Then sliding window sum of size k+1. Yes.

Now, let's think about if "relative order of all the meetings should stay the same" means if we move a block of k meetings to the left, the block remains after meeting a and before meeting a+k+1, so order with all meetings preserved. Yes.

Let's maybe derive from another perspective: Original free gaps are "slack" between meetings. Moving a meeting can transfer slack from one side to the other. With k consecutive meetings moved, you can pool k+1 slacks into one gap. This is intuitive.

Now, let's revisit the upper bound one more time with a potential counterexample involving moving a meeting not adjacent to target gap but changing the target gap through propagation? Suppose target gap between M_i and M_{i+1}. If we move M_{i-1} left, does it allow M_i to move left more even if M_i is unmoved? No, M_i unmoved means its start fixed; gap between M_{i-1} and M_i increases, not target. If M_i moved left, M_{i-1} moved left can help but must be contiguous. If M_{i-2} moved left but M_{i-1} unmoved, M_i can still move left only by f_{i-1} due to M_{i-1}. So non-contiguous moved meetings don't help target gap. Similarly, moving M_{i+2} right but M_{i+1} unmoved doesn't help target gap. Thus upper bound.

What if we move M_i left and M_{i+1} right, and also move some meetings inside the left block not contiguous? The left block must be contiguous ending at i to allow shift; if there is an unmoved meeting inside, it blocks. So moved count for left shift equals length of contiguous moved suffix ending at i. If there are additional moved meetings further left separated by unmoved, they don't help and only consume k, so ignoring them gives a larger or equal bound. Thus maximum gap uses at most two contiguous blocks adjacent to the gap. Good.

Let's maybe formulate the exact relation between shift and gaps:
For a contiguous moved block l..i with l-1 unmoved (or l=1), the maximum left shift of meeting i is sum_{t=l-1}^{i-1} f_t. Is it possible to shift meeting i left by more than this by also compressing durations? No, because original start_i = end_{l-1} + f_{l-1} + d_l + f_l + ... + d_{i-1} + f_{i-1}. Final start_i cannot be less than end_{l-1} + d_l + ... + d_{i-1} (meetings adjacent and after l-1). Difference = sum gaps. So max. Good.
For right block i+1..r with r+1 unmoved, max right shift of meeting i+1 is sum_{t=i+1}^{r} f_t. Original start_{i+1} = start_{r+1} - (d_{i+1}+f_{i+1}+...+d_r+f_r). Final start_{i+1} cannot be greater than start_{r+1} - (d_{i+1}+...+d_r). Difference = sum gaps. Good.

Then final gap G_i = final_start_{i+1} - final_end_i. Let original end_i = e_i, original start_{i+1}=s_{i+1}, f_i=s_{i+1}-e_i.
G_i = (s_{i+1}+delta_{i+1}) - (e_i+delta_i) = f_i + delta_{i+1} - delta_i.
If left block l..i moved, delta_i >= -sum_{t=l-1}^{i-1} f_t (could be less negative? Actually delta_i is at least that lower bound, so -delta_i <= sum). If right block i+1..r moved, delta_{i+1} <= sum_{t=i+1}^{r} f_t. Then G_i <= f_i + sum_right + sum_left. Count gaps = (i-l+1?) Let's count: left gaps l-1..i-1 count i-l+1 = a. right gaps i+1..r count r-i = b. plus f_i =1. Total a+b+1 = (i-l+1)+(r-i)+1 = r-l+2. Moved count = a+b = r-l+1. So total gaps = moved_count+1 <= k+1. Good.

For boundary gap before first: G_0 = f_0 + delta_1. If right block 1..r moved, delta_1 <= sum_{t=1}^{r} f_t. G_0 <= sum_{t=0}^{r} f_t, count r+1 = moved_count+1 <= k+1. After last similarly.

Thus any final gap <= some sum of at most k+1 consecutive gaps. Since all gaps nonnegative, this <= max sum of exactly k+1 consecutive gaps (if fewer, extend to k+1; if at boundary, extension possible because total gaps n+1 >= k+1? Let's ensure no syntax issues:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxFreeTime(self, eventTime: int, k: int, startTime: List[int], endTime: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(startTime)
        gaps = [0] * (n + 1)
        gaps[0] = startTime[0]
        for i in range(1, n):
            gaps[i] = startTime[i] - endTime[i - 1]
        gaps[n] = eventTime - endTime[-1]

        width = k + 1
        current = sum(gaps[:width])
        ans = current
        for i in range(width, n + 1):
            current += gaps[i] - gaps[i - width]
            if current > ans:
                ans = current
        return ans
```
If width = n+1, `sum(gaps[:width])` sums all; loop `range(n+1, n+1)` empty. Good.

Actually if X large, the block shifts left, so its original right portion becomes free. The length increase equals X. The total free gap after block = original f_r + X. This equals sum of gaps f_{l-1}..f_r. It does not include full durations, only the shifted portion. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "duration" of meetings not appearing in formula; surprising but correct because total free time in a span is sum of gaps, independent of durations. Moving meetings just rearranges occupied intervals within the span; the amount of free space in the span is fixed. To maximize a contiguous free gap, compress occupied intervals to one side, yielding free gap equal to total free space in the span. Total free space in span from before first moved meeting to after last moved meeting equals sum of original gaps around/between them. Durations cancel. Good.

Let's write final code:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxFreeTime(self, eventTime: int, k: int, startTime: List[int], endTime: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(startTime)
        gaps = [0] * (n + 1)
        gaps[0] = startTime[0]
        for i in range(1, n):
            gaps[i] = startTime[i] - endTime[i - 1]
        gaps[n] = eventTime - endTime[-1]

        width = min(k + 1, n + 1)
        current = 0
        for i in range(width):
            current += gaps[i]
        ans = current

        for i in range(width, n + 1):
            current += gaps[i] - gaps[i - width]
            if current > ans:
                ans = current

        return ans
```
If width = n+1, loop range(width, n+1) = range(n+1,n+1) empty. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a hidden issue with "gaps" list length n+1 and "width = min(k+1, n+1)" if k+1 > n+1, width n+1; if
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxFreeTime(self, eventTime: int, k: int, startTime: List[int], endTime: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(startTime)
        gaps = [0] * (n + 1)

        gaps[0] = startTime[0]
        for i in range(1, n):
            gaps[i] = startTime[i] - endTime[i - 1]
        gaps[n] = eventTime - endTime[-1]

        width = min(k + 1, n + 1)

        current = 0
        for i in range(width):
            current += gaps[i]

        ans = current

        for i in range(width, n + 1):
            current += gaps[i] - gaps[i - width]
            if current > ans:
                ans = current

        return ans
```
